But what if secrecy is actually the reason for all that strife? And what would happen if we removed that secrecy? What if openness actually increased the sense of fairness and collaboration inside a company? What would happen if we had total pay transparency? For the past several years, I've been studying the corporate and entrepreneurial leaders who question the conventional wisdom about how to run a company. And the question of pay keeps coming up, and the answers keep surprising. It turns out that pay transparency, sharing salaries openly across a company, makes for a better workplace for both the employee and for the organization. Okay, so David, you've written about companies that have actually tried this in the real world. For instance, you looked at this internet startup called Buffer. So can you tell me about them? Like, how did they handle salary transparency? So in Buffer's case, it's a... a formula that they use that takes into account experience, the job that you're doing, the city that you live in, so cost of living adjustment, et cetera, their startup, so whether or not you chose to take equity or not. All of those things get plugged into a formula, and then that formula spits out what the salary is going to be. And so you know, okay, I need to do these things if I want to make that number higher. So what do they do? They have like a whiteboard in their, I don't know, their water cooler, their lunchroom. It's like, here is what everybody gets paid, and you can just see that? It's not a whiteboard. I think it's an intranet and everybody can look up the formula. They actually went what I would call sort of super transparent and posted it on a blog on their website at one point. So you could literally see here's what every single person in the company gets paid. They have a commitment to transparency in that regard that they share it openly, which I also don't advocate every company does. Well, how did that benefit Buffer? How did that make life be easier or better for the employees or for the company? So what I think that transparency does is it says, here's what we value. Here's the formula that we're using to calculate that. So you can say, here's how to make more money in the company. We're going to be open and honest about the differences between people and the differences between salaries. And so when the word of Buffer's commitment to transparency started spreading around, they got inundated with resumes and applications because I think in the back of a lot of people's minds, they don't actually trust that their employer. is looking out for them. And they often can sort of think, maybe they're trying to screw me out of some money. And Buffer can't do that. They've handcuffed themselves to doing what's right by their employees because they're transparent about it. Keeping salaries secret leads to what economists call information asymmetry. This is a situation where in a negotiation, one party has loads more information than the other. And in hiring or promotion or annual raise discussions, an employer can use that secrecy to save a lot of money. Imagine how much better you could negotiate for a raise if you knew everybody's salary. Economists warn that information asymmetry can cause markets to go awry. Well yeah his honours even of a more conventional kind have been poured on Richard Flanagan from his first novel Death of a River Guide to the latest. first person. But the one that's received most awards and reached most readers around the world is The Narrow Road to the Deep North. It's the story of an Australian doctor, Dorigo Evans, who during World War II is taken prisoner by the Japanese, and along with tens of thousands of other allied prisoners, not to mention at least three times as many civilians from around Southeast Asia, is forced to help build a railway line from Thailand to Burma, now Myanmar. A massive undertaking ordered by the Japanese emperor, it's made even more gruelling by the fact that equipment's rudimentary, food scarce, disease rife, and punishment by both Japanese soldiers and Korean guards extreme. The death toll mounts by the hour. In his work camp, Dorigo struggles to save lives and maintain the men's spirit, and returning home after the war finds himself acclaimed a hero. Indeed, he seems to have survived with honour. But Dorigo is doubly haunted. First, by what he saw and experienced in the steaming Thai jungle and all the lives he failed to save. Second, by the memory of an illicit pre-war love affair, a passionate sexual relationship with his uncle's wife. What kind of a hero is he? The Crown Season 2 is set to be coming back to Netflix with its highly anticipated follow-up. Ahead of the new series, here are the 10 most important moments from Season 1. Netflix will be dropping all 10 episodes of Season 2 later this week as the saga of the House of Windsor continues. Claire Foy will be resuming her mantle as Queen Elizabeth II, while Doctor Who star Matt Smith is returning as Prince Philip. Season 1 of The Crown was created and written by Peter Morgan and introduced viewers to the world of the British Royal Family. The first season started in 1947 and ran through until 1955 as the young Princess Elizabeth ascended to the throne. Here are the 10 key plot points from The Crown Season 1. Elizabeth and Philip's Wedding Elizabeth and the Duke of Edinburgh tie the knot in 1947 after the latter renounces his titles and royalty as the Prince of Greece and Denmark. The couple prepare for many years of wedded bliss as they live in Malta, where Philip has been posted as an officer of the Royal Navy. They have two children, Charles and Anne, and are enjoying a simple life on the island away from the strife of royal life. However, Their happiness is shattered when they are brought back to London as Elizabeth's father takes a turn for the worst. King George VI dies. The crown opens starkly with King George VI, played by Jared Harris, coughing up blood, an ominous sign of things to come. His health then rapidly deteriorates as doctors inform him that he has lung cancer with only months left to live. He gives Prince Philip some advice about taking care of his daughter before she becomes the Queen. Despite trying his best to hang on and prepare his young daughter for the arduous task ahead of her, King George VI sadly passes away. The royal couple learn of the sudden death while they are on a tour of the Commonwealth on behalf of the King. Elizabeth's Coronation The Princess who didn't expect to become a monarch for at least 20 years, is thrust into the spotlight. In the first televised British coronation in history, Elizabeth is anointed the Queen at the age of just 25. Prince Philip finds himself increasingly emasculated by his wife and must bend to her will as convention dictates. Alex Jennings, who abdicated from the British throne in 1936 to marry American divorcee Wallace Simpson, ruffles feathers when he arrives in London for the funeral of his brother. Unsurprisingly, he gets a frosty reception from his now estranged family, who are still angry for choosing his lover over them. Even though he doesn't get on with most of them, David, as he is known simply to his family, is on good terms with Elizabeth and offers her advice. He is outwardly kind towards her but is more cynical behind closed doors and constantly criticizes the royal family for their ill-treatment of him and his wife. David and Wallace, Leah Williams, flit in and out of the first season of The Crown as fans get their take from the outcasts from the hallowed British institution. Queen Elizabeth and Prince Philip's marriage troubles. Her ascension to the throne leaves Philip isolated as he's had to leave behind his job in the navy to serve as her consort. While Elizabeth attend official duties and functions, Prince Philip visits gentlemen's clubs and spends his time in lunches and evening soirees with the implication that he could be playing away from home. Tensions grow between the pair and they constantly argue and fight as one point having a heated exchange in a car. Another moment sees the couple fighting as Elizabeth hurls objects at her husband, which is captured by photographers in an embarrassing moment for the royals. Luckily, Elizabeth manages to convince the media to destroy the footage and give the other world the impression that all is well between them. Philip also develops a case of the green-eyed monster when the Queen gives Lord Porchay Porchester, Joseph Kloska, a direct line to her at Buckingham Palace. Princess Margaret and Peter Townsend Along with the Queen's marriage problems, her sister Princess Margaret, Vanessa Kirby, adds to her woes with her affair with Group Captain Peter Townsend, Ben Miles. Elizabeth is torn as she wants to bless the Union but cannot due to royal protocol and objections from Parliament because her lover is a divorced man whose spouse is still living. In a bid to try to appease the government and hope for a marriage down the line, towns and agrees to go on government work in Brussels for two years. After waiting two years, Princess Margaret is informed that she can marry the wrath captain but only if her renounces her titles and royal privileges. The prospect gives Margaret pause for thought and she ends the relationship, leading to much relief from the royal family and parliament. Her decision creates a gulf between Elizabeth and Margaret, and the latter cannot forgive her older sister. Winston Churchill. Prime Minister Winston Churchill, John Lithgow, returns to power after six years of a Labour government and the Queen's first Prime Minister. Winston conceals his ill health from Elizabeth after he is tasked with an international summit with US President Dwight D. Eisenhower when the Soviet Union detonates a H-bomb. She eventually finds out that Winston has been hiding his stroke from her and gives him a scolding for lying to her. The Prime Minister takes centre stage later on in the series as he forms a bond with artist Graham Sutherland, Stephen Delane, who has been commissioned to create a portrait to mark Winston's 80th birthday. Winston, who is also an artist, constantly paints a pond in his garden which is later revealed to be a metaphor for the loss of his daughter Marigold. Marigold died at the age of two and a half and Winston is haunted by her tragic death from tonsillitis. Despite a tentative friendship forming between the two men, Winston is angered by the portrait that depicts him as old and frail. The painting is later destroyed in secret. The Great Smog Winston and the Queen's relationship has its ups and its downs as he refuses to act during the Great Smog of 1952, which sees London engulfed in thick, black blanket of poisonous gases that kills thousands. Elizabeth is pressurized by those around her to act and tell Winston to step down for his lack of inaction. At the very last moment Winston realizes the devastating effects in the wake of his secretary Venetia Scott's, Kate Phillips. death and finally put measures in place. The Great Smog of 1952 leads to the Clean Air Act 1956 and an awareness of pollution and the connection to health. Anthony Eden As Winston grows more frail, he eventually agrees to step down as Prime Minister and allows Anthony Eden, Jeremy Northam, to take over. However, Anthony causes problems as he gets into a heated crisis over the ownership of the Suez Canal with Egypt's President Gamal Abdel Nasser. The new prime minister is not well either and suffers from ill health which requires a cocktail of medication to help him along. Prince Philip's role In a bid to make Philip feel like he has a role, Elizabeth asks him to open the Melbourne Olympics in 1956. She also asks him to go on a tour of the Commonwealth on her behalf that will see him traveling for five months and away from his family. Philip initially seems quite pleased with the opportunity to become a more public figure. Follow up. Ahead of the new series, here are the 10 most important moments from season 1. Pascal's law says that a pressure change, like, for example, adding the weight of more fluid to one part of the container, will be transmitted without loss through the entire fluid to all parts of the container equally, because it's an incompressible fluid. For all the celebrations surrounding the first manned lunar landing of Apollo 11 in 1969, What is forgotten today is just how close they and the other Apollo missions flew to the edge of disaster. Apollo 11 came within seconds of aborting the landing because they had overshot the landing area and were running low on fuel. This was only one of several issues with the Apollo missions that arose that could have led to the loss of the mission and the crew though as it turned out only Apollo 13 came close to this actually happening. This does obviously exclude the Apollo 1 accident where the crew died because of a fire as they rehearsed the launch on January 27th 1967. Apollo 12 was struck by lightning twice during the launch and although it knocked out several electrical systems the main navigation system continued to work and the rest of the mission proceeded successfully. On Apollo 16 the engine backup system malfunctioned on the command module as it orbited the moon. which resulted in a flight being one day shorter but mission control determined that they could work around the fault instead of aborting the mission. Even Apollo 11 was just a test flight it was building upon the previous missions results and achievements. It was to be the first attempt at a manned landing and was driven as much by the desire to achieve President Kennedy's directive to put a man on the moon before the end of a decade as it was to beat the perceived threat of the Soviets getting there first. US national pride was at stake if either of these had failed. As Apollo 11 was to be the first landing NASA wanted to make sure that it would be as event free as possible so they chose to land on the largest flattest part of the moon they could find officially known as site 2 which was a 10 mile long elliptical area in the northern Sea of Tranquility. The idea was the flight computer on board the lander would guide it down from 50,000 feet to 500 feet above the surface where Neil Armstrong would then take over the controls for the final landing. However, Armstrong became aware early on that things were not going to plan and that they'd already flown over the landing site and were heading to a bolder strewn area now known as West Crater about the size of a football field. He assumed control from the computer and manually flew over West Crater and another smaller crater before landing on the outer edge of site two about four miles from its intended target and uttering the immortal words, as he was making the final descent mission control estimated that they had just 25 seconds of fuel left at the time they landed although this was later revised to 45 seconds after the mission. NASA's rules required that the mission be aborted if there were less than 20 seconds of fuel left. because if they didn't there was a danger that they might not be able to perform an emergency ascent. To abort the mission they would have to start an emergency ascent protocol which would separate the ascent module from the lander which they would then fly back to the command module. It would take between two and four seconds for the emergency abort to complete and the ascent engine to fire. All the time they would still be traveling alongside the descent stage. If they didn't get far enough away from the descent stage then there was the danger of any remaining rocket fuel exploding when it crashed into the surface and the flying debris could hit and damage the escape. For all the celebrations surrounding the first manned lunar landing of Apollo 11 in 1969,
[08:07:18.040 -> 08:07:46.510]  What is forgotten today is just how close they and the other Apollo missions flew to the edge of disaster. Apollo 11 came within seconds of aborting the landing because they had overshot the landing area and were running low on fuel. This was only one of several issues with the Apollo missions that arose that could have led to the loss of the mission and the crew though as it turned out only Apollo 13 came close to this actually happening.
[08:07:47.310 -> 08:08:16.410]  This does obviously exclude the Apollo 1 accident where the crew died because of a fire as they rehearsed the launch on January 27th 1967. Apollo 12 was struck by lightning twice during the launch and although it knocked out several electrical systems the main navigation system continued to work and the rest of the mission proceeded successfully. On Apollo 16 the engine backup system malfunctioned on the command module as it orbited the moon.
[08:08:16.760 -> 08:08:46.670]  which resulted in a flight being one day shorter but mission control determined that they could work around the fault instead of aborting the mission. Even Apollo 11 was just a test flight it was building upon the previous missions results and achievements. It was to be the first attempt at a manned landing and was driven as much by the desire to achieve President Kennedy's directive to put a man on the moon before the end of a decade as it was to beat the perceived threat of
[08:08:46.710 -> 08:09:16.950]  the Soviets getting there first. US national pride was at stake if either of these had failed. As Apollo 11 was to be the first landing NASA wanted to make sure that it would be as event free as possible so they chose to land on the largest flattest part of the moon they could find officially known as site 2 which was a 10 mile long elliptical area in the northern Sea of Tranquility. The idea was the flight computer on board the lander
[08:09:17.110 -> 08:09:46.830]  would guide it down from 50,000 feet to 500 feet above the surface where Neil Armstrong would then take over the controls for the final landing. However, Armstrong became aware early on that things were not going to plan and that they'd already flown over the landing site and were heading to a bolder strewn area now known as West Crater about the size of a football field. He assumed control from the computer and manually flew over West Crater and another smaller crater
[08:09:47.100 -> 08:10:20.260]  before landing on the outer edge of site two about four miles from its intended target and uttering the immortal words, as he was making the final descent mission control estimated that they had just 25 seconds of fuel left at the time they landed although this was later revised to 45 seconds after the mission. NASA's rules required that the mission be aborted if there were less than 20 seconds of fuel left.
[08:10:20.790 -> 08:10:47.690]  because if they didn't there was a danger that they might not be able to perform an emergency ascent. To abort the mission they would have to start an emergency ascent protocol which would separate the ascent module from the lander which they would then fly back to the command module. It would take between two and four seconds for the emergency abort to complete and the ascent engine to fire. All the time they would still be traveling alongside the descent stage.
[08:10:48.370 -> 08:10:59.510]  If they didn't get far enough away from the descent stage then there was the danger of any remaining rocket fuel exploding when it crashed into the surface and the flying debris could hit and damage the escape.
